# PRFlow detailed system reference

> **Role of this document.** This is the retained detail reference behind the concise
> [system overview](architecture/system-overview.md). It contains deep mechanics and historical
> context for maintainers. Its positioning section (§21 of this document) remains the messaging
> source of truth that `CLAUDE.md`'s marketing convention points to; agents should begin with the
> overview and follow the focused page for the surface they are changing.
>
> **Provenance & accuracy.** Every fact here was extracted from the PRFlow source
> repository (`The01Geek/prflow`): the README, the skill definitions
> (`skills/*/SKILL.md`), the agent definitions (`agents/*.md`), the GitHub Actions
> workflows (`.github/workflows/*.yml`), the composite actions, the config schema
> (`.prflow/config.schema.json`), and the docs (`docs/internal/*.md` and
> `docs/external/`). Where a number,
> file path, or named mechanism appears, it is quoted as-is so decks/videos don't
> drift from reality. Internal/strategy material (the private roadmap) is
> deliberately **excluded**.
>
> **Version.** This document carries no version literal on purpose — a hand-maintained
> one drifts silently, which is exactly what the accuracy promise above forbids. The
> shipped version is the `version` field of [`.claude-plugin/plugin.json`](../../.claude-plugin/plugin.json),
> and [`CHANGELOG.md`](../../CHANGELOG.md) records what changed in each release.

## Withheld from this release: the automatic pull-request-triggered review tier

**The automatic review on pull request is not shipped in this release.** A fresh
installation receives none of `.github/workflows/devflow-review.yml`,
`.github/workflows/devflow-runner.yml` or `.github/workflows/telemetry-push.yml`, and
`install.sh` no longer copies them.

**Why.** The tier's caller triggered on `pull_request`, `pull_request_target`, `check_run`,
`workflow_run`, `check_suite` and `status`, called a reusable workflow with
`secrets: inherit`, checked out the pull-request head, and carried no actor-authorization
gate. Two open defects describe the consequences and neither is close to landing:

- [**#930**](https://github.com/The01Geek/prflow/issues/930) — the `precheck` job
  performs a bare `actions/checkout`, which under the `pull_request` trigger resolves the
  pull request's merge ref. The config that decides whether a review runs at all therefore
  comes from the pull request under review, so "it defaults to off" is not a mitigation.
- [**#920**](https://github.com/The01Geek/prflow/issues/920) — blocked on #930.
  It is unknown whether the collaborator-permission API call succeeds under `precheck`'s
  `pull-requests: read` token, and a fork `pull_request` event receives a read-only
  `GITHUB_TOKEN` regardless of the `permissions:` block, so the job cannot post the required
  check and the context goes unreported.

**A repository collaborator commenting `/prflow:review` is the always-available review
path**, and this change does not edit it. A repository collaborator with write, admin or
maintain permission comments `/prflow:review` on a pull request; `devflow.yml`'s `gate` job
authorizes the actor through `scripts/authorize-actor.sh`, and the review runs. **An outside
fork contributor cannot self-trigger a PRFlow review — a repository collaborator must post
the comment.** A consumer can *additionally* opt into having that comment posted
**automatically once CI is green** with the documented `pull_request` snippet in
[`workflow-triggers.md`](workflow-triggers.md) (authorized by the same `allowed_bots` gate).

**If you already installed the tier, you keep it.** `install.sh`'s
`prune_stale_devflow_workflows()` is deliberately not extended, so re-running the installer
leaves the three files in place and your auto-review keeps working. That continues to hold
across a plugin upgrade only because **every helper those workflows call is still shipped**
even though the withheld tier no longer reaches them (and, save `derive-review-verdict.sh` —
which the shipped dead-run verdict-presence gate reaches again, issue #1172 — nothing else in
PRFlow's own tree reaches them either): `install.sh` re-stamps
`prflow_version` to the installed commit, so re-running the installer keeps your workflow
files while vendoring a newer plugin, and a helper deleted as "unreachable" would go missing
underneath them — `finalize_check` fails **closed** when `derive-review-verdict.sh` is absent,
which would report every review `incomplete` and wedge every pull request behind a required
check that never reports, while an absent `derive-review-preconditions.sh` fails **open** and
silently drops the freshness and CI-green gates. That is why those helpers are retained rather
than swept. It also means such a repository **remains exposed to the #930 and #920 defects for
as long as `workflows["prflow-review"]` is `true` in its `.prflow/config.json`**. Removing the tier
is a manual step:

1. Delete `.github/workflows/devflow-review.yml`, `.github/workflows/devflow-runner.yml`
   and `.github/workflows/telemetry-push.yml`.
2. Set `workflows["prflow-review"]` to `false` in `.prflow/config.json`.
3. Remove the `Devflow Review` context from any branch protection rule or ruleset that
   requires it — otherwise every subsequent pull request wedges against a required check
   that nothing will report.

The removed caller's bytes are preserved on the `preserved/auto-review-tier` branch, whose
`PRESERVATION.md` records the `devflow-runner.yml` object ID it was cut against. Re-shipping
the tier is a **reconstruction against whatever that callee says at that later time**, not a
restore.

## 1. The one-sentence pitch

**PRFlow makes agentic coding work on real codebases, turning a one-line request into a complete, tested, reviewed, documented pull request that's ready for a developer's final review.**

Out-of-the-box coding agents are dazzling on a demo repo, then you point one at a real ticket in a large, business-grade codebase and it comes back half-done: wrong patterns, missing tests, stale docs. PRFlow is the end-to-end development-workflow plugin for [Claude Code](https://code.claude.com) that closes that gap: it turns a rough idea into a codebase-grounded issue, then orchestrates the full lifecycle, plan, implement, test, review, fix, document, and audits its own review before handing you the PR. Then it closes the loop with a weekly self-improvement pass that reads its own track record and proposes the smallest change that would prevent its next mistake.

---

## 2. The problem PRFlow solves

**The wedge.** AI coding agents can write code, and they look incredible doing it on a fresh, small project. Point one at a real ticket inside a large production codebase and it stalls: it doesn't know the existing patterns, plans against greenfield assumptions, implements half the change, skips the tests, leaves the docs stale, and hands you something that *looks* done but isn't. The hard part of shipping software was never writing code; it's the *workflow around* the code, and that workflow is exactly what breaks down at production scale:

- Turning a vague request into a crisp, buildable spec grounded in *this* codebase.
- Planning against the existing architecture instead of greenfield assumptions.
- Writing the tests, including the test automation the change actually needs.
- Reviewing the change rigorously, not rubber-stamping.
- Fixing what the review finds, then re-reviewing.
- Keeping internal docs, customer docs, and release notes in sync.
- Doing all of this *consistently*, every time, regardless of how big or small the change is.

**What you'd reach for instead, and what it leaves on your plate:**

| Instead of PRFlow | What it leaves you to do |
|---|---|
| Raw coding agents (Claude Code alone, Cursor, Copilot, Devin) | Great on toy projects; on a real codebase *you* still spec, plan, test, review, fix, and document, the agent only drafted part of the code. |
| The stock `@claude` GitHub App | Answers questions and writes code, but enforces no lifecycle, no plan gate, no review-and-fix loop, no docs, no acceptance-criteria check. |
| The manual senior-engineer loop | It works, at the cost of your most expensive people doing spec → plan → implement → test → review → fix → document by hand, inconsistently, on every ticket. |
| Merging the agent's first attempt | The fastest path to a PR that looks done, passes nothing, and resurfaces in production. |

Most "AI writes your code" tools stop at the first step and hand you a diff. PRFlow automates the **entire** loop and treats "committing code as the halfway point, not the finish line."

**The deeper insight (good for a keynote slide).** A single LLM pass is variable: it can miss things, rationalize its own work, or declare victory early, and that variance is exactly what sinks it on a large codebase, where the margin for a half-finished change is zero. PRFlow's architecture is built around *not trusting any single pass*: it uses independent verification checklists, multiple specialized reviewers, a structurally-independent "shadow" re-review to audit its own approval, and a weekly retrospective that audits the whole system. The product thesis is **disciplined, auditable AI software delivery at production scale**: not just code generation.

---

## 3. What PRFlow is, concretely

PRFlow is distributed as a **Claude Code plugin**. The repository is also its own plugin **marketplace**. It bundles:

- **Skills**: the user-facing commands (`/prflow:implement`, `/prflow:review`, the `/prflow:docs` family, `/prflow:create-issue`, `/prflow:retrospective-weekly`, etc.). Each is a `SKILL.md` file containing the procedure the model follows; a larger skill may split its detailed per-phase procedure into on-demand reference files that the `SKILL.md` reads at each step — as `/prflow:implement` does, where a thin orchestrator `SKILL.md` reads a phase's whole ordered set of `phases/phase-N-*.md` files at the start of that phase through a **boundary-gated, fail-closed** entry gate (a self-naming marker pair per file, plus a part-of-N line that establishes the set arrived whole, judged by an accept-or-reject taxonomy — the boundary half only, no content-hash identity layer — §7) and a phase file in turn reaches a **predicate-gated** `references/*.md` file only when a durable predicate says that procedure applies (Phase 4.0's deferred-AC follow-up filing, gated on `scripts/workpad.py deferred-presence` — issue #815; and Phase 4.0.5's deferred-review-finding filing, gated on `scripts/discover-deferral-manifests.py --presence-for-pr <N>` — issue #1374; each loads its reference on the unestablished answer as well as the positive one); as the shared review engine does, where a state-owning root reads a gated `phases/*.md` reference through an identity-and-boundary entry gate (§8); and as `/prflow:create-issue` does, where a thin root reads a marker-gated `references/*.md` file at each step entry and, separately, loads a fallback reference only when that arm's predicate fires (§11).
- **Agents**: fourteen specialized first-party subagents — `checklist-generator`, `checklist-deduper`, `checklist-verifier` power the review engine; the five Phase-3 review agents `code-reviewer`, `silent-failure-hunter`, `comment-analyzer`, `pr-test-analyzer`, `type-design-analyzer` do the bulk of `/prflow:review`'s work (vendored from Anthropic's pr-review-toolkit plugin under Apache-2.0, retained at `LICENSES/pr-review-toolkit-LICENSE`); `code-explorer` (codebase discovery) and `code-architect` (planning) power `/prflow:implement` (vendored from Anthropic's feature-dev plugin under Apache-2.0, retained at `LICENSES/feature-dev-LICENSE`); and four PRFlow-native implement-phase agents share the orchestrator's own checkout — `branch-setup` (Phase 1.4 branch resume/creation), `issue-claim-auditor` (Phase 1.6 issue-claim pre-checks), and `ac-claim-verifier` / `ac-evidence-verifier` (Phase 3.4 acceptance-criteria verification, the latter the only one permitted to run an in-environment verification command). The count matches the file population under `agents/`. PRFlow owns all the vendored agents as a hard fork.
- **Scripts** (`scripts/`, `lib/`), deterministic helpers in Bash, `jq`, and Python that do all the mechanical work (fetching context, computing patterns, gating, git/PR mechanics) so the LLM is only invoked for genuine judgment.
- **Cloud workflows** (`.github/`), optional GitHub Actions that make PRFlow run autonomously on issue/PR events.

It declares **zero companion-plugin dependencies** — every external asset the engine once dispatched is now a first-party PRFlow file, internalized as a hard fork across three seams:

- The five Phase-3 review agents (`code-reviewer`, `silent-failure-hunter`, `comment-analyzer`, `pr-test-analyzer`, `type-design-analyzer`) and the discovery/planning subagents `code-explorer` / `code-architect` are first-party agents under `agents/` (vendored from Anthropic's `pr-review-toolkit` and `feature-dev` plugins under Apache-2.0, retained at `LICENSES/pr-review-toolkit-LICENSE` and `LICENSES/feature-dev-LICENSE`), dispatched as `prflow:<name>` — so neither `pr-review-toolkit` nor `feature-dev` is a dependency. (The pre-rename `devflow:<name>` spellings remain accepted as `agent_overrides` keys — see the roster section below — but the engine dispatches the `prflow:` identifier.)
- The final-pass reviewer (`requesting-code-review`) and the fix-loop `receiving-code-review` principles are first-party skills under `skills/` (vendored from the `superpowers` plugin under its MIT license — Jesse Vincent — retained at `LICENSES/superpowers-LICENSE`), dispatched/invoked as `prflow:<name>` — so `superpowers` is no longer a runtime dependency. (The `writing-skills` authoring discipline is **not** vendored — it remains the external `superpowers` skill, a development-time tool PRFlow's own contributors use, not something the engine dispatches.)

PRFlow owns all the vendored agents and skills as a hard fork, and may modify them from this point forward. With the `superpowers` seam internalized there are **no companion-plugin install dependencies left at all** — PRFlow installs and runs on its own.

The "zero companion-plugin dependencies" claim is scoped precisely: it means the **consumer `/plugin install`** resolves with no `claude-plugins-official` prerequisite, and PRFlow's **engine** (everything under `skills/` + `agents/`) dispatches no companion plugin. It is *not* a claim that the cloud workflows install nothing else — `devflow.yml`, `devflow-implement.yml`, and `devflow-runner.yml` still install `code-review@claude-plugins-official` and `claude-md-management@claude-plugins-official` to make the **ambient** `/code-review` and `/revise-claude-md` slash commands available inside the runner. Those are convenience commands the engine never dispatches as part of a review or implementation, so they are not engine dependencies and do not affect the consumer install. Since issue #505 the cloud workflows additionally honor consumer-declared `enabledPlugins` and `github`-kind `extraKnownMarketplaces` entries from the trusted base-ref `.claude/settings.json` (composed into the same `plugins`/`plugin_marketplaces` inputs by `scripts/resolve-extra-plugins.sh`) — a consumer-chosen convenience mirroring what Claude Code already honors locally, **not** an engine dependency: the engine dispatches none of those plugins, and the entries are composed only from a trusted ref — the maintainer-committed default-branch checkout on the two write tiers, and the materialized base-ref copy on the review tier (which never reads the PR-head checkout's settings file).

`/simplify` (used for self-review) is a **built-in** Claude Code skill, intentionally *not* a dependency.

---

## 4. The two tiers: local and cloud

This distinction is central to every pitch, lead with it.

### Local tier (zero config, no infrastructure)
The skills you run **inside Claude Code**. Works with **no configuration file at all**: every setting has a built-in default. You type `/prflow:implement 42` (or `/prflow:review`, etc.) in your editor and it runs in your session. Requirements: `git`, `gh` (GitHub CLI, authenticated), `jq`, and Python 3.11+; PyYAML is an advisory extra on this tier (a missing PyYAML degrades one helper rather than blocking, and `bash lib/preflight.sh` reports it but still exits 0). A `bash lib/preflight.sh` verifies the environment. Shell helpers avoid GNU-only flags, so macOS/BSD work without GNU coreutils. The toolchain is also self-defending on Windows / non-UTF-8 hosts: a committed `.gitattributes` forces every `*.sh`/`*.py`/`*.jq` to LF on checkout regardless of `core.autocrlf` (so a shebang never becomes `bash\r`), and every first-party `scripts/*.py` **and `lib/*.py` command** forces its own streams and `gh` I/O to UTF-8 (so an em-dash or emoji never trips a cp1252 codec); a derived write-side guard in `lib/test/test_python_scripts.py` enforces that stream-forcing over the whole population — tracked Python commands (a module-body `if __name__ == "__main__":` block) under `scripts/` and `lib/`, excluding `lib/test/`, with no tracked command outside those two directories — deriving its checked file list from the repository index (`git ls-files`) so a newly added helper is covered without editing the test (`scripts/stale-prose-lint.py` is a declared exception, forcing UTF-8 the replace-not-raise way so an undecodable byte degrades one character rather than aborting a scan). A distinct second layer covers **local file inputs**: the helpers that read a local text file the caller prepared — `parse-acs.py --body-file`, `workpad.py`'s `_read_section_file` (serving `--replace-plan-file`/`--replace-acs-file`/`--set-reproduction-file`, via a shared `_decode_utf8` helper), and `branch-for-issue.py --title-file` — decode that file **explicitly** as `encoding="utf-8"` rather than the ambient locale codec, so UTF-8 issue text survives on a non-UTF-8 default host (where issue #222's stream hardening alone would still let `Path.read_text()` mis-decode the file it reads). A decode or OS failure routes through the parser's, workpad's, and branch-create's existing clean non-zero paths — an actionable diagnostic, no traceback, no partial output, and on the workpad path no GitHub PATCH is issued. An AST guard over tracked `scripts/*.py` (in `lib/test/test_python_scripts.py`) fails the suite on any new ambient-codec `read_text`/`Path.open`/builtin `open` text read, accepting keyword or positional `encoding` and excluding binary mode. On a stock Windows Python install (where Python is on PATH only as `python`/`py -3`, with no `python3`), run `bash scripts/provision-python3-shim.sh --apply` once to install a consent-gated `python3` shim that forwards to the first `>=3.11` interpreter — preflight points you there, and it is a no-op on macOS/Linux where a real `python3` already exists. External binaries are resolved with the same execution-verified discipline through a shared resolver family: `lib/resolve-bin.sh` is the single source of truth for the generic selection contract (`devflow_resolve_bin <tool>`), sourced by `lib/preflight.sh` (which only DETECTS) and — via thin per-tool wrappers — by every helper that USES the tool, mirroring their sibling `lib/resolve-python.sh`. The contract: an explicit, non-empty `DEVFLOW_<TOOL>` override (`DEVFLOW_GH`, `DEVFLOW_JQ`) wins outright with no probe; otherwise the first of `<tool>`/`<tool>.exe` whose network- and auth-free `<tool> --version` actually runs is selected (rejecting a present-but-unrunnable Windows/WSL shim); if neither runs, the bare tool is returned with a stderr breadcrumb naming the override remedy (always exit 0). `lib/resolve-gh.sh` delegates to it for `gh` (kept as its own file because every gh-calling helper sources it by name; the `DEVFLOW_GH` contract is unchanged, and the seven Python gh-callers — `workpad.py`, `file-deferrals.py`, `match-deferrals.py`, `parse-acs.py`, `export-workflow-lifecycle-census.py`, `build-experiment-records.py`, `preflight.py` — honor the same override), and `lib/resolve-jq.sh` is the `jq` sibling, sourced by every jq-calling helper to resolve `jq` once into `DEVFLOW_JQ`. `scripts/run-jq.sh` is the agent-tier sibling of that helper for the `jq` an agent composes inside a `SKILL.md` body (every executable agent-composed `jq` site — the retrospective family plus the cloud-governed `implement`, `docs-release-notes`, and `review` skill bodies): because each skill-body `jq` runs in a fresh per-Bash-call agent shell where `DEVFLOW_JQ` is neither exported nor persisted, the source-once-then-reuse idiom does not translate, so those skills invoke `run-jq.sh` **by path** (`"${CLAUDE_SKILL_DIR:-<absolute skill base directory this runner reports in context>}"/../../scripts/run-jq.sh`, or the vendored `.prflow/vendor/prflow/scripts/run-jq.sh` the cloud allowlist grants) in place of bare `jq` — it sources the shared `resolve-jq.sh` and execs the resolved binary, selecting a runnable `jq` on a shim-shadowed Windows/WSL host exactly as the helper tier does (a transparent no-op elsewhere; degrades to bare `jq` with a breadcrumb on a partial deployment). `lib/preflight.sh` execution-verifies both `gh` and `jq` with a two-branch diagnosis (not installed vs. present-but-unrunnable, each naming the override remedy); `install.sh` alone carries an inline jq adaptation because it runs standalone before any checkout exists (there a broken `DEVFLOW_JQ` warns and routes to the python3 arm). A companion helper, `lib/normalize-path.sh` (`devflow_normalize_path`), converts a Windows-form path (`C:\...`) to the running shell's POSIX form — `wslpath`, else `cygpath`, else an environment-detected translation (`/mnt/c/...` under WSL, `/c/...` under MSYS/Git Bash), else unchanged with a breadcrumb. The skill preambles no longer paraphrase that whole chain: since issue #1856 they instruct the agent to run only the helper's `wslpath`/`cygpath` probe at prompt time and then validate the located directory against the filesystem (`ls <candidate>/../../scripts/`), with no drive-letter-arithmetic fallback (the anchor is what locates `lib/`, so it cannot source the helper it bootstraps — see §6). On macOS/Linux/cloud, where the bare tools run, every resolver returns the bare name on the first probe — no behavior change. The **bash that RUNS** those `.sh` helpers is a level above the resolver family: it is chosen at the invocation boundary (the agent/runner that shells into bash) before any `.sh` executes, so a sourced resolver cannot select it (bootstrap — the resolver would itself need a chosen bash to run). PRFlow supports **any** POSIX bash — WSL bash, Git Bash, or MSYS2 bash, none mandated — and the invocation layer honors a **`DEVFLOW_BASH`** override to point at one. Consistent with its detect-not-select posture, `lib/preflight.sh` is a diagnostic here too: under bash it emits a `devflow-bash:` breadcrumb (interpreter path + `$BASH_VERSION`) and surfaces `DEVFLOW_BASH` when set; run under a non-bash POSIX shell (empty `$BASH_VERSION`) it prints a remedy naming the three supported bashes + the override and exits non-zero before the first bash-only `${BASH_SOURCE[0]}`, failing closed rather than aborting with a cryptic "Bad substitution" (a host with no POSIX bash at all — PowerShell-only — is out of scope). See `CONTRIBUTING.md` for the residual Windows caller-side execution discipline.

### Cloud tier (autonomous, optional)
GitHub Actions workflows make PRFlow run **autonomously on issue/PR events**. You comment `/prflow:implement 42` on an issue and the workflow drives the whole lifecycle without you in the editor. It needs only one secret **by default**, `CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN`, and a little setup (see `docs/internal/cloud-setup.md`); routing a workflow through an optional third-party model provider adds one more, `DEVFLOW_PROVIDER_API_KEY`. No GitHub App required.

**Both tiers can run on one repo** without conflict. The local marketplace copy is cached centrally; the cloud tier materializes its own copy of the plugin at runtime.

---

## 5. The end-to-end workflow (the headline demo)

This is the canonical story for a demo video or "how it works" slide:

```
   you: a rough idea
        │
        ▼
  ┌───────────────────┐   /prflow:create-issue
  │ 1. Create issue   │   turns the idea into a structured GitHub issue
  └───────────────────┘   (asks clarifying questions, you confirm before it files)
        │
        ▼
  ┌───────────────────┐   comment  /prflow:implement <#>  on the issue
  │ 2. Trigger        │   (a human comment starts it)
  └───────────────────┘
        │
        ▼
  ┌───────────────────┐   devflow-implement.yml runs /prflow:implement autonomously:
  │ 3. Implement      │   branch → plan → code → tests → draft PR → /simplify →
  └───────────────────┘   /prflow:review-and-fix → docs → marks the PR ready
        │
        ▼
  ┌───────────────────┐   devflow-review.yml posts a /prflow:review verdict as a
  │ 4. Review gate    │   PR check; you review and merge
  └───────────────────┘
```

**Narration points:**

1. **Create the issue.** `/prflow:create-issue Add CSV export to the reports page`. PRFlow interviews you until the issue is unambiguous, shows you the rendered draft, and files it **only after you confirm**. Say it lands as **#42**.
2. **Trigger implementation.** Comment `/prflow:implement 42` on the issue. Because *you* (a real user) posted it, GitHub fires the workflow natively, no bot, PAT, or GitHub App needed. (Note: the trigger is the bare `/prflow:*` form, **not** `@claude`, that prefix is ceded to Anthropic's Claude GitHub App.)
3. **PRFlow implements it.** The workflow creates a branch, plans against your codebase, writes code and tests, opens a **draft** PR, self-reviews with `/simplify`, runs `/prflow:review-and-fix`, files follow-up issues for any deferred findings, updates the docs, and flips the PR to **ready**.
4. **Review and merge.** A second workflow runs `/prflow:review` as a gate and posts its verdict as a PR check. A human does the final review and merges.

**Prefer the editor?** Run `/prflow:implement 42` directly in Claude Code, both reach the same lifecycle.

---

## 6. The skill catalog

| Skill | What it does | How it's invoked |
|---|---|---|
| `/prflow:implement <issue#>` | Full lifecycle: fetch issue → branch + workpad → discover/plan → implement → test → draft PR → `/simplify` → `/prflow:review-and-fix` → acceptance gate → file follow-up issues → docs → ready PR | interactively, or by commenting on an issue (cloud) |
| `/prflow:review [PR#]` | Verification-checklist-driven review; runs the first-party `prflow:` review agents + the first-party `prflow:requesting-code-review` final-pass reviewer; returns **APPROVE/REJECT** (no auto-fix) | interactively, or a bare `/prflow:review` comment on the PR |
| `/prflow:review-and-fix [PR#]` | `/prflow:review` + an automatic fix loop (max **4** iterations); writes a deferrals manifest at Loop Exit | interactively; called by `/prflow:implement` Phase 3 |
| `/prflow:pr-description [issue#]` | Generate/update PR description from the branch diff; renders the Scope-Acknowledged Findings block | interactively; called by `/prflow:implement` Phase 4 |
| `/prflow:docs` | Orchestrates the three doc steps in one session | interactively; called by `/prflow:implement` Phase 4 |
| `/prflow:docs-sync-internal` | Update internal docs to match branch code changes | called by `/docs` |
| `/prflow:docs-sync-external` | Align external/customer docs with internal docs | called by `/docs` |
| `/prflow:docs-release-notes` | Generate a release-note entry for customer-visible changes; on a version-bump branch, also reconcile that version's CHANGELOG entry (any PR, not just customer-visible) | called by `/docs` |
| `/prflow:docs-verify <topic>` | Verify/refresh internal docs for one topic (has `--report-only` mode, which also accepts an explicit search-space operand) | interactively; dispatched as a peer subagent by `/prflow:create-issue` Step 1 |
| `/prflow:docs-bootstrap-internal` | Stand up an internal-docs structure from scratch | interactively |
| `/prflow:docs-bootstrap-external` | Generate initial external docs from internal docs | interactively |
| `/prflow:create-issue` | Rough idea → well-structured GitHub issue | interactively |
| `/prflow:init` | One-time setup: scaffold `.prflow/config.json` + refresh schema | interactively |
| `/prflow:retrospective-weekly` | The weekly self-improvement loop orchestrator | interactively |
| `/prflow:retrospective` | Stage A: per-PR retrospective analysis | subagent only |
| `/prflow:retrospective-audit` | Stage B: per-pattern issue-spec drafting | subagent only |

**Agents** (`agents/`): `checklist-generator`, `checklist-deduper`, `checklist-verifier`.

**Naming note (important for accuracy in any demo):** PRFlow's commands use the `prflow:`-namespaced form. This matters most for names that collide with built-ins: `/review`, `/init`, and `/security-review` are *built-in* Claude Code commands, so always use `/prflow:review` and `/prflow:init` to reach PRFlow, especially from GitHub Actions comments, where the namespaced form is required.

**Init on an upgraded repository — the bounded migration and the opt-in rename sweep.** On a repository set up before the PRFlow rename, `/prflow:init` first runs the deterministic, all-or-nothing DevFlow→PRFlow layout migration (state directory, vendored path, config keys, workflow bodies, marketplace source), then — **only** after that migration reports terminal `APPLIED` — *offers* a consent-gated, model-driven **semantic sweep** that finds and repairs stale `DevFlow` product-name prose the mechanical rename map cannot classify. The offer is separate from the deterministic migration (unchanged) and from the protected-literal map (`lib/rename-map.json`, never widened by the sweep), and it discloses model access to tracked, untracked, and **git-ignored** file contents — ignored files can hold secrets — *before* asking for consent, so a declined or non-interactive run makes no sweep writes. The canonical, exhaustive statement — disclosure text, candidate enumeration, exclusions, bounded resumable ledger, pinned authority, atomic candidate writes, the preserve-by-default semantic predicate, incomplete handling, reporting, and the renewed-consent resume arm — lives in [`install.md`](install.md) → *Migrating a repository set up before the PRFlow rename*; this paragraph is the non-authoritative summary.

### Extending skills with prompt extensions (consumer-owned)

Every skill in the catalog honors one upgrade-safe extension convention. The bundled reader is

```bash
"${CLAUDE_SKILL_DIR:-<absolute skill base directory this runner reports in context>}"/../../scripts/load-prompt-extension.sh <skill-name>
```

which prints the contents of `.prflow/prompt-extensions/<skill-name>.md` (resolved relative to the repo root — see the `DEVFLOW_PROMPT_EXTENSION_ROOT` override below) when that file exists, and the skill treats that text as **additional instructions appended verbatim to the end of its own prompt** for that run. `<skill-name>` is the skill's directory name under `skills/` (`create-issue`, `implement`, `review`, …) and is the reader's only **positional** argument. When the file is **absent** — or present but empty — the helper prints nothing **on stdout** and the step is a **no-op** for the appended prompt: the skill behaves exactly as it does without it. A file that exists but cannot be delivered (unreadable, a broken symlink, or not a regular file) is refused loudly with a non-zero exit and a breadcrumb, and the skill surfaces that rather than silently skipping it — so a misconfigured extension never vanishes without a trace.

**Whole-file mode reports its own resolved status on stderr (issue #1299).** On a successful (exit-0) **whole-file** load — no `--section` — the helper additionally emits one `load-prompt-extension.sh: PROMPT-EXTENSION-STATUS: <content-present|present-empty>` line on **stderr**, reusing `scripts/render-prompt-extension.sh`'s status-token strings: `content-present` when a non-empty file's bytes were printed to stdout, `present-empty` on the absent/empty no-op arm. Stdout stays **byte-verbatim** (the token never touches it), and the point of the token is that an absent/empty extension is now distinguishable from a **harness refusal**, which produces no output at all — so a reader can tell "the consumer shipped no extension" from "the load never ran". The undeliverable shapes above still exit before the token, and `--section` mode emits no token, so `create-issue`'s byte-and-stderr contract is unchanged. The review-engine bodies consume this: `skills/review/SKILL.md` and `skills/review-and-fix/SKILL.md` report the token verbatim as the extension's resolved status on their success arms, and a total absence of the line (no output or a non-zero exit) is read as **unestablished**, never collapsed onto `present-empty` (*unknown is not zero*).

**How the extension reaches the agent: one channel, the loader ladder (issues #1264, #1462, #1472).** The paragraph above describes the *reader*. Which mechanism *delivers* its output is a separate question, and for `/prflow:review`, `/prflow:review-and-fix` (both its blocks — its own extension and `receiving-code-review`), `/prflow:implement` and `/prflow:pr-description` — **five** call sites across four skill bodies — the answer is a single channel: each body invokes the `load-prompt-extension.sh` ladder itself, unconditionally, and nothing else delivers consumer policy into it. The ladder's three rungs are the granted vendored literal `.prflow/vendor/prflow/scripts/load-prompt-extension.sh <skill-name>`, the same helper with the `.prflow/vendor/prflow/` prefix removed, and the portable `${CLAUDE_SKILL_DIR:-…}` anchor form; `lib/test/lint-anchor-fallback-arm.py` enrols those call sites and fails a body that emits the anchor with no vendored fallback arm.

**Re-loaded at each re-entry boundary, not only at run start (issue #1574).** The ladder result is a `Bash` tool output with no re-attachment on context compaction, so a load lost partway through a long run leaves the rest of that run without consumer policy and nothing re-fetches it. The four surfaces that re-enter themselves therefore re-invoke their ladder at each existing re-entry boundary in addition to the run-start load: `implement` at every phase (re-)entry and mid-phase re-anchor, `review` at every phase entry and shadow entry, and the fix loop once per iteration for both its `review-and-fix` and `receiving-code-review` ladders. Each re-invocation refreshes already-loaded policy rather than issuing a fresh directive (so imperative content is not re-executed per re-entry), and a refused or non-zero re-load is surfaced at that boundary rather than deferred. `/prflow:pr-description` is genuinely single-pass and re-invokes nowhere. This is recovery against eviction, not a token saving — it adds tokens per re-entry. The re-load timing does not weaken the sole-delivery-channel rule above.

**The render-time placeholder channel is retired (issue #1472).** From issue #1264 until #1472 each of those bodies also carried a render-time `` !`…` `` placeholder invoking `scripts/render-prompt-extension.sh <skill-name>`, which Claude Code's preprocessor executed **before the model saw the skill**, substituting its stdout. It was added because on the prose path alone the load reached the agent in only **8 of 18** sampled review runs and **1 of 4** sampled implement runs, both failure modes silent. Issue #1462 answered that by making the ladder unconditional at every site, which left the placeholder redundant where it worked — and a forensic audit of run `31287654057` then established that it is worse than redundant: **a `Skill`-tool load of any body carrying a placeholder fails outright**, returning `is_error=true` whose entire content is a permission-refusal string and no skill body at all (`prflow:implement` 208 chars; `review-and-fix` and `pr-description` 218 chars each), while the two skills without one (`prflow:docs`, `simplify`) loaded clean. The correlation was exact. The cause is the phase mismatch `CLAUDE.md` records — the injection runs as a preprocessor *during* skill loading, while `allowed-tools` authorizes tool calls *after* — so no grant fixes it, and the refusal surfaces as a `Skill` tool result rather than a `permission_denied` event, making the whole failure class invisible to `permission_denials_count`. `scripts/render-prompt-extension.sh` and its `Bash(*/render-prompt-extension.sh:*)` grants are retained (narrowing the read-only `review` profile is a security-boundary change needing a same-PR `lib/review-profile.tokens` move); only the placeholder lines are gone, and `lib/test/modules/prompt-extension-reader.sh` pins their absence per site so a reflexive restoration goes RED.

**On an implement run**, each surface's outcome is recorded on the workpad as a nested `## Progress` row (`prompt extension resolved: …`), one per surface, written by `scripts/workpad.py`'s template whether or not the run cooperates — a row's *existence* is deterministic, its *tick* is the run's own report, issued by the implement orchestrator from an implement phase file. **That tick keys on observed content, never on an exit status (issue #1472):** the row is ticked only when the ladder's *full* output reached the agent and either carried the extension's contents or was empty, and the ladder is therefore run with no `>/dev/null` and no `| head -<n>`. Run `31287654057` ticked `prompt extension resolved: implement` off `exit=0` from a command that sent the vendored arm to `/dev/null` and truncated the repo arm to `head -3` — three lines of a 341-line extension reached the agent, and the extension's *Batched artifact regeneration* section went undischarged across three full-suite launches. The authoritative statement of the rule is the Extension-row tick rule in `skills/implement/phases/phase-1-setup.md` §1.3. The standalone `/prflow:review`, `/prflow:review-and-fix` and `/prflow:pr-description` paths have no workpad: they fetch unconditionally and record nothing.

**The implement tier enforces arrival independently of that self-report (issue #1446).** The workpad tick above is the run's own report on the channel under test, so a lost skill body — which a `Skill`-tool load aborts carrying only a refusal string and no body, invisibly to `permission_denials_count` — could tick nothing yet still terminate `Complete`. `devflow-implement.yml` therefore brackets the agent step with two **job-level** steps driving `scripts/prompt-extension-arrival.py`: a pre-agent `Classify prompt-extension arrival` reads the extension root **directly** — the same root the ladder resolves (`DEVFLOW_PROMPT_EXTENSION_ROOT` when set and non-empty, else the repo-root `.prflow/prompt-extensions/`), recorded so the run states which root it read — and emits the expected state (`arrived-expected`, `absent`, or an `undeliverable-*` fault) as a step output; a post-agent `Reconcile prompt-extension arrival` checks that independent expectation against the run's durable workpad, deriving `unestablished` where deliverable content was expected but no ticked `prompt extension resolved: implement` row records its arrival — the exact shape a lost load produces, since a lost body can tick no row. On `unestablished` the step fails the job with an `::error::` (which restates that `permission_denials_count` does not cover this class), forces the non-arrival record onto the workpad, and flips its Status off `Complete`; an `absent` extension (the majority adopter state) or a reconciled `arrived` is left clean.

Every failure path fails **closed** — an absent detector (a pinned `prflow_version` predating the change), an unreadable workpad Status or body, or a classify step that recorded no expectation while the agent reported success each **block** rather than pass, because a value that could not be established is not evidence the body loaded. The steps run outside the tool matcher, so they need no `lib/capability-profiles.json` grant; being resolved from the default branch at trigger time the surface is post-merge-only and cannot be observed by its own PR's implementing run — the helper is proven at its own input boundary by `lib/test/modules/prompt-extension-reader.sh` instead.

**Issue #1971 extended the same enforcement to the tiers #1446 left uncovered.** `devflow.yml` now brackets its agent step with the same **job-level** steps `devflow-implement.yml` carries: a pre-agent classify that reads the extension root from the **trusted base-ref closure** the workflow already materializes (`DEVFLOW_PROMPT_EXTENSION_ROOT`, never the PR-head checkout — see the base-ref trust boundary in §15), records the expected token, and records `detector-absent` when the helper is absent from both the vendored and repo-root paths — that pre-agent step **warns and exits 0**, never failing the job itself, so the classification survives to the reconcile rather than aborting the run before the agent has even started; and a post-agent reconcile that refuses to let a run whose agent reported success terminate clean when the expectation cannot be reconciled. **`detector-absent` is one of those unreconcilable expectations, so the post-agent step DOES fail the job on it** (matching `devflow-implement.yml`'s #1446 pair): the state means an older vendored `prflow_version` whose skill body may carry none of this enforcement, and letting such a run post a green verdict is exactly the unverified-clean-terminal this surface exists to prevent. The remedy a consumer in that version skew applies is bumping the vendored `prflow_version`, which the `::error::` names. The **local/interactive and cloud-review tiers have no workpad tick and no job step to stat the root on the local side**, so `scripts/prompt-extension-arrival.py` gained a `classify-ladder-output` mode that classifies from the delivery ladder's **own emitted `PROMPT-EXTENSION-STATUS:` line** rather than from a checked-out root. It is **positive-signal only**: `arrived` only on a produced `content-present` status, `absent` only on a produced `present-empty` status, and `unestablished` whenever no recognized status line was produced at all — which is exactly what a permission denial of a helper invoked by path (no output) looks like, so a denied invocation is never read as `arrived` by default. The three workpad-less skill bodies (`skills/review/SKILL.md`, `skills/review-and-fix/SKILL.md`, `skills/pr-description/SKILL.md`) write an `unestablished` finding to a **fixed durable-record fallback order** — the workpad, else the PR description, else the run's own terminal output naming the state and reporting the record unrecordable — as a forced write, not an instruction the agent may skip. To let a cloud review/command-tier run execute the prose-side classifier, `scripts/prompt-extension-arrival.py` is granted in the **`command`** profile in `lib/capability-profiles.json` (the five generated allowlist literals regenerated, `manifest_version` bumped once); the read-only **`review`** profile is deliberately **not** widened — it is a security boundary locked by `lib/review-profile.tokens`, so on that tier the invocation is denied, yields no output, and is classified `unestablished` by the positive-signal rule rather than reported as arrival.

**Which directory the reader reads (`DEVFLOW_PROMPT_EXTENSION_ROOT`, issue #874).** The reader has **two** directory-selection branches. When `DEVFLOW_PROMPT_EXTENSION_ROOT` is set and non-empty it names the extension directory outright, so the composed path is `<root>/<skill-name>.md` with **no** `.prflow/prompt-extensions/` segment appended and **no** `git rev-parse` call; that branch writes a stderr breadcrumb naming the directory it resolved. Otherwise the reader takes the repo-root branch described above — the only branch the issue-#295 repo-root-reader enumerations (in `.prflow/config.schema.json` and `scripts/emit-git-env.sh`) are about. The variable is top precedence and inert both when unset **and** when set empty, the same `DEVFLOW_GH` / `DEVFLOW_JQ` / `DEVFLOW_BASH` convention, so every caller that leaves it unset sees byte-identical stdout and exit codes. **Who may write the file and which checkout supplies its bytes are different questions.** The extension is consumer-owned on every tier; but on the cloud review paths the bytes a repo-root read would return are pull-request content — the automated runner because it checks out the PR head, and the shipped `devflow.yml` command tier because `/prflow:review-and-fix`'s branch sync moves the working tree there mid-run — so both set this variable to a `$RUNNER_TEMP` closure materialized from the trusted base ref. See the **base-ref trust boundary** bullet in §15 (Security model), the canonical statement of that boundary, of the two tiers' deliberately different mechanisms, and of the residuals it does not close.

**Loading one section instead of the whole file (`--section`, issue #611).** A skill that re-loads its extension repeatedly — `/prflow:create-issue` re-loads at four points, deliberately, because context compaction can evict the turn-one text and a heading-less extension makes that loss invisible — paid a full-extension dump into context every time, when each consumption site wanted exactly one section. The reader therefore accepts an optional `--section '<## heading>'` after the positional: `load-prompt-extension.sh create-issue --section '## Audit dimensions'`. A section spans its heading line to the next line beginning `## ` (two hashes **plus a space**, so a `###` sub-heading is section content and terminates nothing), else to end of file; duplicate same-heading sections concatenate in file order; a `##` line inside an open HTML comment block or inside a fenced code block is never a heading, and an unclosed fence runs to end of file. Both CommonMark fence characters are tracked (` ``` ` and `~~~`), and a fence closes only on its own kind. The comment rule is scoped by *where the line begins*: a line that **begins** `## ` is a heading whatever trails it, including an inline comment (`## Evidence axes <!-- consumer note -->`), while a line that merely contains `<!--` without starting `## ` (`<!-- ## Commented -->`) is inert — testing the comment shape first would make an inline-annotated heading unselectable *and* make the absent-heading breadcrumb enumerate the file as though a heading the consumer can plainly see were not there. Comment-block state is resolved by the **last** marker on each line, so `<!-- a --> <!--` leaves the block open for the lines that follow; trailing whitespace is stripped from both the candidate heading line and the flag's value before comparison, so a CRLF-authored extension still extracts. At most one section per invocation — a repeated flag takes its last occurrence. The extraction is **bash-builtin-only** (no `awk`/`sed`/`tr`/`head`): the emitted section decides what the consuming skill sees, and under the repo's non-preflight-PATH-tool rule such a value must not be derived through a tool `lib/preflight.sh` does not guarantee, which would empty the selection silently rather than failing loudly.

Two contracts keep the flag safe to adopt. A **flagless** invocation stays byte-identical to the full-file behavior above, so every pre-existing caller is untouched. And the two ways a section can yield nothing are held apart: an **absent heading in a non-empty extension** stays the designed no-op (exit 0, empty stdout) but additionally emits a stderr breadcrumb naming the requested heading and the headings actually present — so a near-miss heading (case drift, a typo) is observable instead of silently contributing nothing — while an **empty section** stays breadcrumb-free, because its heading *was* found and there is nothing to fix. Malformed flag usage is refused loudly at exit 2 rather than silently reverting to the full dump: an unrecognized `--`-prefixed argument; a `--section` missing its value, empty after stripping, or given a **flag-shaped** value (`--section --bogus`, the shape a dropped heading argument leaves); a `--`-prefixed token in the skill-name slot (a transposed flag, which would otherwise look up a skill literally named `--section` and no-op at exit 0); and a **heading-shaped bare positional** (`load-prompt-extension.sh create-issue '## Audit dimensions'` — a dropped `--section`, which would otherwise emit the *whole* extension at exit 0, the exact opposite of what the caller asked for and indistinguishable at the call site from a legitimate full-file load). Each refusal closes a shape that would otherwise select silently; a bare **plain word** extra argument keeps its pre-existing ignored behavior. This `--section` flag remains the sibling extraction implementation for its own callers; since issue #600 the create-issue audit hooks are owned by `scripts/render-audit-prompt.py`, and the extraction rule is **specified** in `skills/create-issue/references/audit-prompt-template.md`.

**Runner-portable anchor (single-statement, generalized to every local-tier skill).** `${CLAUDE_SKILL_DIR}` is exported by Claude Code but is *empty* on other agentic CLIs (confirmed on Copilot CLI v1.0.67; expected on Cursor, Codex CLI, Gemini CLI, …), where the bare `${CLAUDE_SKILL_DIR}/../../scripts/…` form collapses to a broken `/../../scripts/…` path. A second defeat stacks on top: Copilot CLI's inline `bash -c` marshaling **strips a variable assigned in one statement and read in a later statement of the same inline command** (verified: `bash -c 'v=hi && echo $v'` prints empty), so the earlier assign-then-reuse recipe (`SKILL_DIR="${CLAUDE_SKILL_DIR:-…}"; … "$SKILL_DIR"/../../scripts/…`) also fails there. Every skill therefore resolves the anchor **inline in the same single statement that invokes the helper**: `"${CLAUDE_SKILL_DIR:-<absolute skill base directory this runner reports in context>}"/../../scripts/…` — preferring `$CLAUDE_SKILL_DIR` when set and non-empty (the `:-` expansion treats empty the same as unset), and otherwise the agent substitutes the placeholder with the skill base directory the runner reports in context (e.g. a `Base directory for this skill:` line), converting a Windows-form path (`C:\…`) to POSIX form first at prompt time with one standalone `wslpath -u`/`cygpath -u` probe — the skills carry no shell normalization mirrors and, since issue #1856, no drive-letter-arithmetic fallback either — then accepting the located directory only once `ls <candidate>/../../scripts/` validates it in the same shell. On Claude Code the resolved value is identical to the bare form, so behavior there is unchanged. If neither `$CLAUDE_SKILL_DIR` nor a runner-reported base directory whose `../../scripts/` validates is available, the skill fails closed (stop + report) rather than running a broken path — except `/prflow:create-issue`, which is deliberately best-effort throughout (an unresolvable anchor never blocks issue creation; a skipped provenance label or prompt-extension load is reported explicitly instead). This is distinct from the cloud-tier carve-out below, where workflows reference the literal vendored path because the sandbox blocks `$`-expansion. **The two skills that resolve `<skill-dir>` as a *value* to base their phase-file reads on — `skills/implement/SKILL.md` and `skills/review/SKILL.md` — invert this order (issue #1594): they take `<skill-dir>` from the runner-reported base directory *first* with no shell command on that path, and the `echo "${CLAUDE_SKILL_DIR:-…}"` command is only their fallback, emitted when the runner reports no base directory.** A refusal of that fallback command is a named third outcome shape distinct from its two output shapes (ran-and-printed-empty, ran-and-printed-placeholder-unsubstituted) — the command never ran, so the `$CLAUDE_SKILL_DIR` channel is *unestablished* rather than empty — and the desk-time lint `lib/test/lint-reported-base-dir-arm.py` enforces that the reported-base-directory-first arm precedes the value-consuming expansion at each enrolled call site.

**A skill loads the extensions its behavior draws on (issue #620).** The usual case is one — a skill's own — but a skill that applies *another* skill's principles without invoking that skill also loads that skill's extension, so the policy follows the behavior rather than the invocation. `/prflow:review-and-fix` is the instance: at entry it loads its own `review-and-fix` extension and then the `receiving-code-review` extension, because the fix loop applies `prflow:receiving-code-review` principles without ever invoking that skill — so a repo's reception policy would otherwise reach direct passes only and silently miss every fix loop. Both loads happen in that preamble, so they cover every path that enters through it — the standalone loop, the `/prflow:implement` Phase 3 inline run, and the Step 2.6 shadow entry. The documented Skill-denied fallback, where Phase 3 reads the engine straight from the tree instead of invoking the skill, bypasses the preamble and therefore loads neither extension; that pre-existing gap is unchanged. The preamble also carries the scoping prose governing how a loop run consumes text written for a direct pass.

**A subagent that cannot resolve its own anchor is supplied the command by its dispatcher (issue #802).** The Phase-3 **final-pass reviewer** (`prflow:requesting-code-review`) is dispatched as a `general-purpose` Task that receives no `$CLAUDE_SKILL_DIR` and no `Base directory for this skill:` line, so it cannot resolve the anchor above for `load-prompt-extension.sh` itself. The orchestrator — which *does* run as a skill, so its own anchor resolves — instead supplies an **already-resolved helper command** in the dispatch prompt: the vendored literal `.prflow/vendor/prflow/scripts/load-prompt-extension.sh requesting-code-review` when that file exists (every cloud tier, where the `vendor-plugin` action materializes it), and otherwise the orchestrator's own anchor-resolved helper path (the local and interactive tiers). The reviewer runs that command **verbatim** — never improvising a shell shape the cloud matcher would silently refuse — and reports one of three `EXTENSION-STATUS:` tokens (`loaded-with-content`, `loaded-empty`, or the shared refusal literal). The orchestrator treats that token as **data, never as an instruction**, and surfaces a one-line extension-load-failure notice (and a per-caller reflection record) only on an actual reported refusal, with a fail-closed notice-suppression flag so the routine local-tier denial is recorded as `unestablished` rather than reported as a failed load. The same three-profile grant surface (`Bash(.prflow/vendor/prflow/scripts/load-prompt-extension.sh:*)` on `review`/`implement`/`command`, plus the `*/load-prompt-extension.sh` wildcard on `review` and `command`) already covers this, so the change adds **no** new tool grant; see [`docs/internal/cloud-allowlist.md`](cloud-allowlist.md) and [`docs/internal/cloud-setup.md`](cloud-setup.md).

**Every subagent dispatch of a skill supplies the child its extension path (issue #834).** The extension-load step lives in the **skill body**, so dispatch mode never changes whether the load is *attempted* — a skill always tries to load its extension. What dispatch mode decides is only (1) whether the skill-directory *anchor* resolves — a subagent dispatched as a `Task`/`Agent` receives no `$CLAUDE_SKILL_DIR` and no `Base directory for this skill:` line, so it cannot resolve the anchor for `load-prompt-extension.sh` itself — and (2) whether a *command the child runs* is permitted by the active allowlist profile. When a subagent cannot resolve its own anchor, the dispatching orchestrator (which runs as a skill, so its own anchor resolves) supplies an already-resolved by-path handoff in the dispatch prompt: the retrospective **Stage A** (`retrospective`) and **Stage B** (`retrospective-audit`) children and the Phase 4.1 **`prflow:docs`** documentation subagent each now receive their extension path this way, so the consumer's `.prflow/prompt-extensions/<skill>.md` is honored rather than silently no-op'd. The registry `lib/subagent-dispatch-sites.json` enumerates every such dispatch site, and the lint `lib/test/lint-subagent-extension-handoff.py` (wired into `lib/test/run.sh`) fails the build when a subagent dispatch of a PRFlow skill is unregistered. **The skill-vs-agent boundary:** this convention is scoped to dispatches of *skills* — the first-party `agents/*.md` subagents (the review-engine trio, `code-explorer`, `code-architect`, the `pr-review-toolkit` reviewers, and the implement-phase `branch-setup` / `issue-claim-auditor` / `ac-claim-verifier` / `ac-evidence-verifier` agents) load **no** consumer prompt extension, because an agent is not a skill and carries no `.prflow/prompt-extensions/<name>.md` contract.

**The extension path was never the only thing a subagent cannot resolve (issue #1333).** The same unresolvable anchor blocks a child from reaching *any* bundled helper or plugin-relative file, so a brief that names one through the anchor names something the child cannot use — and, on the two retrospective children, the anchor rule's own `stop and report` failure arm would put prose on a stdout contract that admits exactly one JSON object, while the config reader it reached for resolves its default path with `git rev-parse`, which those briefs forbid. Stage A (`skills/retrospective/SKILL.md`) therefore resolves **nothing**: `skills/retrospective-weekly/SKILL.md`'s Step 4 resolves the bundled-helper root and the internal-documentation root once before the dispatch loop — alongside the repository root it already resolved — and substitutes all three into the dispatch prompt by value, where the brief consumes them as `[[PLUGIN_ROOT]]` and `[[INTERNAL_DOC_LOCATION]]`. Stage B (`skills/retrospective-audit/SKILL.md`) got the identical treatment in issue #1338 — its Step 8b dispatch resolves the bundled-helper root and hands it over by value, and the brief consumes it as `[[PLUGIN_ROOT]]` (Stage B reads no internal-documentation root, so it needs only that one value). The claim that a by-path handoff needs no permission grant is **per value**, not blanket: a value the child only *reads* needs none, but `[[PLUGIN_ROOT]]` names a helper the child *executes*, so the child's tier must permit that invocation. The shared *Portable helper anchor* paragraph stays in the brief as the byte-identical 17-way coupled copy, preceded by a paragraph scoping it out of that file; `lib/test/run.sh`'s `#275` P3 presence pin and the `lpe-coverage` loader enumeration both invert to **absence** assertions for each declared file (Stage A, and Stage B since #1338), so the removed call sites stay guarded rather than merely unchecked.

The extension file lives in the **consumer's** repo, committed under `.prflow/prompt-extensions/` and shared by the team. It is never part of the plugin, so marketplace updates never overwrite it and never conflict with it — the same consumer-owned-vs-plugin separation that keeps your `.prflow/config.json` yours across updates (note the extension files are *tracked/committed* so a team shares them — as is the live `config.json`, which is committed so the cloud tier reads it from the tree, not gitignored). `/prflow:init` scaffolds the directory with a commented, inert `<skill-name>.md.example` for **every** skill — each carrying a skill-specific hint — so adopters discover that the convention works for all of them, not just one. The examples are backfilled per file (each created only when absent), so an adopter who scaffolded earlier picks up any newly added examples on re-run while their own extension files (an edited `.example`, or a live `<skill-name>.md`) are never overwritten. Re-running the scaffolder also **skips creating a `<skill-name>.md.example` when a live `<skill-name>.md` already exists** for that skill: once an adopter has activated an extension, dropping a redundant `.example` beside it would just be confusing clutter, so the live `.md` (read-only here, never modified) suppresses its own example. A pre-existing `.example` is likewise left untouched. The helper validates the skill-name argument (rejecting any value containing `/` or `..`) before any filesystem access, so the resolved path can never escape `.prflow/prompt-extensions/`.

**Worked example — Azure DevOps test cases in every generated issue.** An adopter who stores test cases in Azure DevOps wants `/prflow:create-issue` to list the applicable test cases in each generated issue body, while upstream PRFlow stays Azure-DevOps-agnostic. They:

1. Register a **project-scoped Azure DevOps MCP server** in their repo's `.mcp.json`.
2. Commit `.prflow/prompt-extensions/create-issue.md` containing, for example:
   > When building the issue body, call the Azure DevOps MCP server to fetch the test cases applicable to this work item and list them under a new "## Applicable Azure DevOps Test Cases" section.

Because the injected text is appended verbatim and MCP tools are model-invoked from prose (not deterministically function-called), the extension must **tell the model to call the tool** rather than assume it runs automatically. No plugin file is edited; the customization is entirely consumer-owned and survives every plugin update.

**`create-issue` audit-dimensions hook (issue #443).** The `create-issue` extension has one **structured** hook the skill reads by heading: a section headed exactly `## Audit dimensions` in `.prflow/prompt-extensions/create-issue.md` is forwarded verbatim to the Step 3.6 fresh-context audit subagent (§11), appended to its generic dimension checklist, so a consumer teaches the auditor the repo-specific premises its issues must respect. Since issue #600 that read + extraction is performed natively in-process by `scripts/render-audit-prompt.py` (not `load-prompt-extension.sh`), whose `render-status:` line reports whether the section was appended. An extension without that heading changes nothing. Since issue #729 a consumer bullet may optionally declare its own coverage key with a `<!-- dim-key: <lowercase-kebab> -->` marker on the line immediately above it (emitted as `c:<declared-key>` by the renderer's `enumerate-dimensions` mode and stripped from the auditor-facing prose); an undeclared bullet keys off its bold-lead name slug, else a hash of its text, so inserting a bullet mid-section no longer renumbers its siblings — but only a declared key survives a reword. The declaration arms are specified once, in `skills/create-issue/references/audit-prompt-template.md`, and deliberately not paraphrased here. Consumers discover the contract from the scaffolded `create-issue.md.example`, which carries an inert sample of the heading; this repo's own live `.prflow/prompt-extensions/create-issue.md` ships the PRFlow-engine audit dimensions, each carrying an explicit declaration.

**`create-issue` evidence-axes hook (issue #548).** A **second** structured hook mirrors the first: a section headed exactly `## Evidence axes` in `.prflow/prompt-extensions/create-issue.md` extends the generic evidence-axis floor the Step 2 independent-derivation *evidence-bundle sub-pass* records (§11), so a consumer adds repo-specific axes the pass must evidence before the approach question is asked. The two hooks are extracted by the **same heading-extraction rule** — since issue #600 owned and implemented once by `scripts/render-audit-prompt.py`'s section-extraction mode and documented in `skills/create-issue/references/audit-prompt-template.md` (the `## Audit dimensions` hook consumes it spliced into a dispatch arm as `{CONSUMER_DIMENSIONS}`, while `extract --hook evidence-axes` exposes the same rule for the evidence-axes hook, whose operative pass-time re-load remains `scripts/load-prompt-extension.sh --section`; that flag also remains the sibling implementation for its own other callers) — and each is appended only at its own consumption site, so an extension carrying both feeds `## Audit dimensions` to the audit dispatch and `## Evidence axes` to the evidence pass independently. The rule is deliberately **not** restated here: it lived at three sites before issue #611 and drifted, so this paragraph references the specification rather than paraphrasing it. A denied or failed pass-time re-load is surfaced with a dedicated unestablished-axes marker, never read as "no extension"; an absent heading in a non-empty extension is likewise reported from the loader's stderr breadcrumb rather than passing silently. The scaffolded `create-issue.md.example` carries an inert sample of this heading too; this repo's live extension ships the PRFlow-engine evidence axes (see the live extension's `## Evidence axes` section for the current axis list — this paragraph deliberately does not enumerate them, so it cannot rot when an axis is added).

---

## 7. Deep dive: `/prflow:implement` (the 4-phase orchestrator)

This is PRFlow's flagship. It runs a **mandatory 4-phase lifecycle** for a GitHub issue, every phase runs regardless of issue size. The orchestrator does most work directly in its own continuous session; it dispatches subagents at the points its dispatch-instructing surfaces name — the implement bundle (branch setup, issue-claim audit, discovery, architecture, acceptance-criteria verification, documentation, PR description, and the interactive-skill wrapper), the Phase 3.3 review engine it runs in its own context, and the consumer prompt extension. Authorization is stated as a **property** over those surfaces rather than a closed list of points (§7's dispatch-authorization surface), so a newly shipped dispatch point does not silently fall outside it. Planning, implementation, testing, and fixing outside an authorized dispatch point stay the orchestrator's own inline work — the sole exception being Phase 3.4's evidence verifier, which is permitted to run an in-environment verification command.

> **Tagline:** *"Committing code is the halfway point, not the finish line."* The PR stays a **draft** until Phase 4.3.

### The phase reads are boundary-gated, fail-closed (issue #1551)

The orchestrator `SKILL.md` is thin: it reads one `phases/phase-N-*.md` file at the start of each phase and follows it, rather than carrying every phase's procedure inline. Because that read is load-on-demand, a read that returns a partial body — or the wrong phase's body — would otherwise be executed as if it were correct, with no signal in the run's own output. Every phase-file read is therefore gated behind a **self-naming boundary marker** and is **fail-closed** for a body the reader cannot deliver. A body the reader can deliver only in pages is not that: the entry gate pages it whole and applies the taxonomy to the assembled document (the *paged-read recovery*; see row 4 below).

Each `phases/phase-N-*.md` reference carries, as its literal **first** line, `<!-- prflow:implement-ref phase=<N> file=skills/implement/phases/<name>.md start -->`, and the matching `end` marker as its literal **last** line. A member of a multi-file phase carries `<!-- prflow:implement-set phase=<N> part=<k> of=<n> -->` as its literal **second** line: an intact marker pair speaks only for its own file, so it is the part-of-N lines that establish the phase arrived whole. After each `Read` the entry gate quotes the body's actual first and last lines and applies an **accept-or-reject taxonomy**, tested in a fixed order — the first that fires is the attributed shape:

| # | Shape | Stop label |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | denied — the `Read` errored or was refused, no body returned | `boundary: denied` |
| 2 | empty — body zero-byte or whitespace-only | `boundary: empty` |
| 3 | missing — both markers absent | `boundary: missing` |
| 4 | truncated — exactly one marker absent | `boundary: truncated` |
| 5 | duplicate — a marker appears more than once | `boundary: duplicate` |
| 6 | reversed — the `end` line precedes the `start` line | `boundary: reversed` |
| 7 | noncanonical — unique and ordered, but not literally first-and-last | `boundary: noncanonical` |
| 8 | misrouted — marker pair present and canonical, but its self-named phase or path is not the file this gate intended to read | `boundary: misrouted` |
| 9 | set-incomplete — every member read cleared the rows above, but the run does not hold every part of this phase's set | `boundary: set-incomplete` |

**Why the split products are siblings registered in the entry gate, and not references a phase file loads.** Anthropic's skill-authoring guidance says to *"Keep references one level deep from SKILL.md. All reference files should link directly from SKILL.md to ensure Claude reads complete files when needed"*, because *"Claude may partially read files when they're referenced from other referenced files … Claude might use commands like `head -100` to preview content rather than reading entire files, resulting in incomplete information"* (Anthropic, *Skill authoring best practices*, `https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/agents-and-tools/agent-skills/best-practices`, retrieved 2026-08-11). This repository already sits at that limit rather than below it: a phase file reaches its own predicate-gated references, which is a second level, and the boundary contract on those reads is what turns a partial read into a visible stop. Relocating phase content into files a phase file loads would add a third level, trading a read that fails loudly for one that fails silently — so the members of a phase's set are registered directly in `skills/implement/SKILL.md` and stay one level below it.

The marker's self-named `file=` path is compared against the **plugin-relative** form (`skills/implement/phases/…`), stripping any vendored or absolute prefix first, so a consumer that reaches the file under a vendored prefix clears the check on a correct file. On any row the gate **stops that phase**, reports the `boundary:` stop label with the phase id and reference path, and does **not** act on the body, improvise the phase from its stub/orientation text, or repair the file. **Repair is out of band** — a human edit or a command other than this one — because a broken marker halts every run of `/prflow:implement`, including a run dispatched to fix it, so a stopped run is told how to get out rather than facing a command that cannot start.

**Row 4 has a size cause the gate now separates from a damaged file, and still prevents upstream (issue #1595).** A phase file larger than the reader can return in one call yields its `start` marker and no `end` marker while intact on disk, so the entry gate pages it whole before the taxonomy runs — a size limit no longer stops the phase on any reader that offers a continuation, and a file still reaching row 4 is either damaged or delivered by a reader that truncates without offering one. As an upstream control the ceiling remains: `lib/test/lint-reference-size.py` caps every boundary-gated reference and skill root at 61,750 bytes — 95% of the reader's 25,000-token cap at the floor of the measured bytes-per-token densities — and fails the suite on a file over it that holds no live exemption in `lib/test/reference-size-exemptions.json`. The two largest phase files were over that line when the check landed and were carried as expiring exemptions; issue #1606 split each of them into an ordered set of siblings that each read whole, which met those rows' expiry condition and retired them. Several other covered files still sit within tens of bytes of the ceiling; the check is described in full in the review-engine section below.

Every row the implement table shares with `skills/review/SKILL.md`'s reference boundary contract (which holds the canonical set), and their fixed test order, are a **required copy** of it. Two rows diverge, and both are named so no divergence goes unstated: **misrouted** makes explicit the mis-routed read the review engine folds into its rows' *expected* qualifier, and **set-incomplete** has no canonical counterpart at all, because a review phase reference is one file while an implement phase routes to an ordered set. The two row sets are edited in the same change. This paragraph and the table above state the divergences by name rather than by count, so adding a row cannot leave a stale number behind. Unlike the review engine (§8), which pairs the boundary half with a content-hash **identity** layer, implement adopts the **boundary half only** — it has **no identity layer**, so a body locally modified while keeping its own correct markers is not detected, and that limit is stated so the guarantee is not read wider than it is.

### The Workpad: the run's single source of truth
PRFlow maintains **exactly one** marker-tagged comment on the GitHub issue for the whole run (marker: `<!-- prflow:workpad -->`, configurable). It is the durable progress surface, the "job started" acknowledgment, and the resume point. It is driven by `scripts/workpad.py`, a stateless CLI.

- **Status glyphs:** 🚀 (in progress) → 🎉 (`Complete`) / 👎 (`Blocked`) / 💥 (`Failed`) / 🛑 (`Cancelled`). The first three drive the **emoji reaction** on the triggering comment (rocket / hooray / -1). **💥 (`Failed`) and 🛑 (`Cancelled`) are workpad-only and have no triggering-comment reaction** — the cloud stall backstop writes 💥 when a run dead-ends and 🛑 when a run is cancelled (issue #498; see the Stall-backstop note below), never the agent, so no outcome reaction is emitted for either.
- **Sections (in order):** marker, header, `Status`, `Branch`, `Run`, `PR`, `Last updated`, `## Progress`, `## Plan`, `## Acceptance Criteria`, `## Reproduction` (bug-report classification only), `## Devflow Reflection`. The bug-only `reproduction captured` `## Progress` row is pre-rendered from the `bug` *label* by the deterministic producers (the cloud `gate` job and `new-body`), then reconciled to the recorded **content** classification by Phase 1.3's `workpad.py update --reconcile-reproduction` (issue #449) — idempotent, run on every entry, and it never removes a *ticked* row or a populated `## Reproduction` section (historical evidence). `## Progress` also carries one nested `prompt extension resolved: …` row per consumer-prompt-extension surface an implement run consumes — `implement` under **Setup**, the review engine, the fix loop and code-review reception under **Review**, and PR description under **Documentation** — rendered by `new-body` from `workpad.py`'s single-source `_EXTENSION_ROWS` (text paired with the stable substring the tick and the reconciliation both detect it by), and repaired into a workpad created before they existed by Phase 1.3's idempotent `workpad.py update --reconcile-extension-rows` (issue #1462). The row's *existence* is deterministic and an unticked one survives `--status Complete`; see the prompt-extension delivery section above for what a tick means.
- **Checkbox ticking is addressable two ways.** `workpad.py update` ticks a `## Progress`/`## Plan`/`## Acceptance Criteria` checkbox either by **unique substring** (`--tick-progress`/`--tick-plan`/`--tick-ac`) or — for Plan and Acceptance Criteria — by **1-based index** within that section (`--tick-plan-n`/`--tick-ac-n`, counting every `[ ]` and `[x]` row in document order; Progress has no index form). The Phase 3.4 acceptance gate ticks ACs by index, so it no longer depends on hand-picked unique prose substrings.
- **A static substring operand must be host-safe (issue #1679).** Git Bash/MSYS rewrites a standalone slash-leading argument (e.g. `/simplify`) into a Windows path before native `python3` receives it, so such an operand ticks nothing and reports a volatile miss on those hosts. The shipped Phase 3.2 tick therefore passes the substring `simplify` — which still uniquely matches the displayed `` `/simplify` `` row — not `/simplify`, and the derived guard in `lib/test/test_python_scripts.py` fails the suite on a static standalone `--tick-progress` operand under `skills/implement/` (quoted or unquoted) that begins with `/` or carries a shell metacharacter; an unquoted or double-quoted shell-variable operand is runtime-resolved and exempt (a single-quoted `'$X'` suppresses expansion, so it is a static literal and stays guarded). See `docs/internal/install.md` for the host guidance.
- **Reflection bullets are grouped by kind.** Inside the collapsed `## Devflow Reflection` `<details>` block, `workpad.py update --reflection-kind {blocked|deferred|dropped-failed|improvement|issue-accuracy|note}` renders each bullet under one of **three** `### ` sub-sections, in the canonical order `### ⚠️ Action required` → `### 💡 Improvements` → `### ℹ️ Notes`: the three actionable kinds (`⛔ Blocked` / `⏭️ Deferred` / `❗ Dropped/Failed`, all **labeled**) under **`### ⚠️ Action required`**; engine/process-improvement proposals (`💡`, **glyph-only**) under **`### 💡 Improvements`**; and wrong/underspecified-issue-claim feedback (`📝 **Issue accuracy:**`, **labeled**) plus informational notes (`ℹ️`, the default, **glyph-only**) under **`### ℹ️ Notes`**. A kind whose heading already names it drops the redundant bold label (issue #476: `note`, `improvement`); the others keep it. Reflection text passed via `--reflection-file PATH` (or stdin `-`) is read verbatim as UTF-8, bypassing shell interpolation for text containing backticks/`$`/quotes. Sub-headings are `### ` (level-3, never `## `) so the retrospective parser is not truncated. The Phase 1.6 issue-claim audit records clean confirmations as `## Progress` `--note`s (not reflections), so a frictionless run leaves `reflections[]` empty and stays cheap. (This restatement documents the reflection-*kind* routing — a workpad-mechanics contract — not the prose-*style* rules the shared writing standard `lib/writing-standard.md` absorbed; it is retained unchanged and does not point at the standard. See `docs/internal/implement-skill.md`'s disposition note, issue #1039.)
- **The workpad answers a bounded predicate, and it is never read as a body to gate on (issue #815).** Phase 2.2.5's scope decisions are stored as hidden `kind=deferred` records on `## Progress` bullets, and Phase 4.0 decides whether to load its follow-up-filing reference by asking `workpad.py deferred-presence <issue> <pr>` — never by reading the workpad body, which at Phase 4 is larger than the procedure being gated. The answer is **three-state, on the exit code** (`grep(1)`'s idiom): **0** `outstanding: <n>` plus one normalized `criterion:` line each; **1** `not-outstanding: <n>`; **2** `unestablished: reason=<token> unbound=<u> corrupted=<c>` — the workpad was unreadable, it does not carry exactly one `## Progress` section, records are still `pr=pending` or bound to a superseded PR, a bound record's payload is corrupt, or the isolated-bullet reader sees fewer records than the whole-body reader `acs-resolve` uses. Phase 4 **loads the reference on both 0 and 2**: an unavailable operand is never read as "nothing was deferred", because that reading strands deferred work silently while a needless load costs one read. The predicate is decided in Python over the record grammar `workpad.py` already owns — never through a `grep`/`tr`/`sed` pipeline, which `lib/preflight.sh` does not guarantee and which would fail *open* exactly where the tool is absent.
